and call it the
X
-gradient
of
\mathcal{F}
.

With
Definition
6.4
in hand we may now turn our attention towards two gradients of the variational energy that we will use throughout: the
\mathring{H}^{1}
and
L^{2}
-gradients.
We record expressions for both of these below.

Lemma 6.5

(
\mathring{H}^{1}
and
L^{2}
-gradients for our variational energy)
.

Consider the variational energy
E_{var}
introduced in
Theorem
6.1
.
The
\mathring{H}^{1}
-gradient
of
E_{var}
is given by

\left(\nabla_{\mathring{H}^{1}}E_{var}\right)(p)={\left(-\Delta\right)}^{-1}\left[-\Delta p-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}\left(\min\left(M-\partial_{3}p,\,0\right)\right)+PV\right],

meaning that
u=\left(\nabla_{\mathring{H}^{1}}E_{var}\right)(p)
is the unique weak solution of

-\Delta u=-\Delta p-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}\left(\min\left(M-\partial_{3}p,\,0\right)\right)+PV

(6.4)

satisfying
\fint_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}u=0
, i.e.
u\in\mathring{H}^{1}
such that, for every
\phi\in\mathring{H}^{1}
,

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\phi=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\nabla p\cdot\nabla\phi+\frac{1}{2}\min\left(M-\partial_{3}p,\,0\right)\partial_{3}\phi+\langle PV,\,\phi\rangle

(6.5)

Moreover, if
p\in H^{2}
,
M\in H^{1}
, and
PV\in L^{2}
then the
L^{2}
-gradient of
E_{var}
is given by

\left(\nabla_{L^{2}}E_{var}\right)(p)=-\Delta p-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}\left(\min\left(M-\partial_{3}p,\,0\right)\right)+PV.

(6.6)

Proof.

Item 3 of
Theorem
6.2
tells us that
u=\nabla_{\mathring{H}^{1}}E_{var}(p)
satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\phi={\left(u,\,\phi\right)}_{\mathring{H}^{1}}={\left(\nabla_{\mathring{H}^{1}}E_{var}(p),\,\phi\right)}_{\mathring{H}^{1}}=DE_{var}(p)\phi

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\nabla p\cdot\nabla\phi+\frac{1}{2}\min\left(M-\partial_{3}p,\,0\right)\partial_{3}\phi+\langle PV,\,\phi\rangle,

which is precisely (
6.5
), the weak formulation of (
6.4
).
In order to establish (
6.6
) it suffices to integrate by parts the expression
for the Gâteaux derivative of
E_{var}
obtained in item 3 of
Theorem
6.2
.
This yields

{\left(\nabla_{L^{2}}E_{var}(p),\,\phi\right)}_{L^{2}}=DE_{var}(p)\phi=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\left[-\Delta p-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}\left(\min\left(M-\partial_{3}p,\,0\right)\right)+PV\right]\phi,

from which the claim follows.
∎

Remark 6.6

(Comparing the
L^{2}
and
\mathring{H}^{1}
gradients)
.

It is not a coincidence that in
Lemma
6.5
above the
L^{2}
and
\mathring{H}^{1}
gradients are related via

\nabla_{\mathring{H}^{1}}E_{var}={(-\Delta)}^{-1}(\nabla_{L^{2}}E_{var})
.
This identity actually follows from the following fact.
For any Hilbert space
H\subseteq L^{2}
and any Fréchet differentiable
F:L^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
,
if we denote by
R_{H}:R\to R^{*}
the Riesz representation map on
H
then

\nabla_{H}F=R_{H}(\nabla_{L^{2}}F)
.
Indeed, for any
u
,
\phi\in H
:

DF(u)\phi={\left(\nabla_{L^{2}}F(u),\,\phi\right)}_{L^{2}}={\langle\nabla_{L^{2}}F(u),\,\phi\rangle}_{H^{*}\times H}={\left(R_{H}(\nabla_{L^{2}}F)(u),\,\phi\right)}_{H}.

In our case we have that
R_{\mathring{H}^{1}}={(-\Delta)}^{-1}
in the sense that,
for any
f\in H^{-1}
,
u=R_{\mathring{H}^{1}}F
if and only if
u
is the
\mathring{H}^{1}
-weak solution
of
-\Delta u=f
in
\mathbb{T}^{3}
.
The general fact above thus tells us that
\nabla_{\mathring{H}^{1}}E_{var}=R_{\mathring{H}^{1}}(\nabla_{L^{2}}E_{var})={(-\Delta)}^{-1}(\nabla_{L^{2}}E_{var})
,
as observed in
Lemma
6.5
.

Besides gradient descent methods using the
\mathring{H}^{1}
and
L^{2}
gradients,
the third descent method we will discuss is
Newton descent
.
This comes from using, locally, the inner product generated by the
Hessian
of the variational energy to define a gradient direction.
This idea is now made precise.

Definition 6.7

(Newton descent)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}:H\to\mathbb{R}
be a twice Fréchet differentiable map over a Hilbert space
H
.
Suppose there exists
C>0
such that, for every
u\in H
,
D^{2}_{u}\mathcal{F}(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)
induces an inner product,
written
{\left(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\right)}_{D_{u}^{2}\mathcal{F}}
and called the
Hessian inner product
, such that

\frac{1}{C}{\left\lvert\left\lvert\phi\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{H}\leqslant{\left\lvert\left\lvert\phi\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{D^{2}_{u}\mathcal{F}}\leqslant C{\left\lvert\left\lvert\phi\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{H}\text{ for every }\phi\in H

(i.e.
{\left\lvert\left\lvert\,\cdot\,\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{D_{u}^{2}\mathcal{F}}
is
uniformly
comparable to
{\left\lvert\left\lvert\,\cdot\,\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{H}
).
The negative of the
gradient
of
\mathcal{F}
with respect to the Hessian inner product is called the
Newton descent direction
.

Having defined Newton descent we now record its form for our variational energy of interest.

Proposition 6.8

(Newton descent for our variational energy)
.

Let
E_{var}
be the variational energy introduced in
Theorem
6.1
.
The
Hessian inner product
induced by
E_{var}
is, for any
p\in\mathring{H}^{1}
and
M\in L^{2}
,

{\left(\phi,\,\psi\right)}_{D_{p}^{2}E_{var}}=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\nabla\phi\cdot\nabla\psi-\frac{1}{2}\mathds{1}(M<\partial_{3}p)(\partial_{3}\phi)(\partial_{3}\psi)

(6.7)

and so the
Newton descent direction

\pi
is the
\mathring{H}^{1}
-weak solution of

-\nabla\cdot\left[\left(I-\frac{1}{2}\mathds{1}(M<\partial_{3}p)e_{3}\otimes e_{3}\right)\nabla\pi\right]=\Delta p+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}\left(\min\left(M-\partial_{3}p,\,0\right)\right)-PV,

(6.8)

for
PV\in H^{-1}
, i.e., for all
\phi\in\mathring{H}^{1}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\nabla\pi\cdot\nabla\phi-\frac{1}{2}\mathds{1}(M<\partial_{3}p)(\partial_{3}\pi)\partial_{3}\phi

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}-\nabla p\cdot\nabla\phi-\frac{1}{2}\min\left(M-\partial_{3}p,\,0\right)\partial_{3}\phi-\langle PV,\,\phi\rangle.

(6.9)

Proof.

Note that
{\left(\phi,\,\psi\right)}_{D_{p}^{2}E_{var}}=D^{2}E_{var}(\phi,\,\psi)
and
so (
6.7
) follows from differentiating the expression for
DE_{var}
recorded in item 1 of
Theorem
6.2
,
since
\min(\,\cdot\,,0)
is Lipschitz with derivative
\min(\,\cdot\,,0)^{\prime}=\mathds{1}(\,\cdot\,<0)
.
Since the Newton descent direction
\pi
is characterised by the fact that
{\left(\pi,\,\phi\right)}_{D^{2}_{p}E_{var}}=-DE_{var}(p)\phi
for every
\phi\in\mathring{H}^{1}
,
(
6.9
) then follows immediately from (
6.7
) and the expression for
DE_{var}
of item 3 of
Theorem
6.2
.
To see that (
6.8
) is indeed the strong form of (
6.9
) we integrate by parts.
∎

Remark 6.9

(Interpretation of Newton descent)
.

Section
6.2
below details how the Newton descent iteration, recorded in
Proposition
6.8
above,
may be interpreted geometrically.

Another interpretation is already available to us now.
First: note that once Newton descent enters the so-called “quadratic regime”, backtracking line search will systematically select steps of unit size.
Given a current iterate
p_{k}
, the next iterate
p_{k+1}
will thus be given by
p_{k+1}=p_{k}+\pi
where
\pi
solves (
6.8
),
which we may rewrite as

-\nabla\cdot\left(A_{H_{k}}\nabla\pi\right)=\nabla\cdot\left(A_{H_{k}}\nabla p_{k}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}\left(H_{k}M\right)-PV

We may rearrange this equation and deduce that the new iterate
p_{k+1}=p_{k}+\pi
solves

\nabla\cdot\left(A_{H_{k}}\nabla p_{k+1}\right)=PV-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}\left(H_{k}M\right).

In other words: the next iterate
p_{k+1}
is obtained by solving the linearised
PV
-and-
M
inversion
where the Heaviside characterising the linearisation comes from the previous step.
Crucially: this is precisely the method that was used in previous works
[
HESS21
,
TS24
]
to numerically solve the nonlinear
PV
-and-
M
inversion.
This means that the convergence rates presented here for Newton descent are applicable to previous works.
Moreover this means that the convergence
guarantees
for Newton descent are also the first convergence guarantees of this numerical method which is already used in practice.

The remainder of this section is devoted to the obtention of convergence rates for each of the three descent methods discussed above.
There are two key assumptions that must be verified about our variational energy in order for such convergence rates to be obtained.
The first comes from convexity and takes the form of a quadratic lower bound on the variational energy.
The second, which takes the form of a quadratic
upper
bound on the variational energy, is that to which we turn our attention now.

The basic ideas are that (1) we will introduce a simpler energy density for which, due to its piecewise quadratic nature, we can easily find such a quadratic upper bound,
and (2) we will show that this simpler energy density can be turned into our true variational energy density up to some affine transformations.
The result will then follow from the fact that such affine transformations will only interact with these quadratic upper bounds in predictable ways.
In order to carry out this argument it is particularly convenient to introduce the notion of a
quadratic remainder
, which we define below.

Definition 6.10

(Quadratic remainder)
.

Consider a continuously differentiable function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
.
For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we call function
T_{x}^{f}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
defined as

T_{x}^{f}(y)\vcentcolon=f(y)-f(x)-Df(x)(y-x)

for every
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
the
quadratic remainder of
f
at
x
.

We now show that the aforementioned “simpler” energy density – introduced in the result below – does indeed have a quadratic upper bound.

Lemma 6.11

(Quadratic upper bound for a piecewise quadratic function)
.

Consider
f:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

f(x)=\frac{1}{2}{\lvert x\rvert}^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\min{\left(x_{3},\,0\right)}^{2}

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
For every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,

f(y)\leqslant f(x)+Df(x)(y-x)+\frac{1}{2}{\lvert y-x\rvert}^{2}.

Proof.

To make the computations easier we introduce the following function:

A(x)\vcentcolon=I-\frac{1}{2}\mathds{1}(x_{3}<0)e_{3}\otimes e_{3}
,
such that

f(x)=\frac{1}{2}A(x)x\cdot x,

and hence

\nabla f(x)=A(x)x
.
In particular note that, for every
x
, the largest eigenvalue of
A(x)
is equal to
1
, and so
A(x)\xi\cdot\xi\leqslant{\lvert\xi\rvert}^{2}
for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

In terms of the
quadratic remainder

T_{x}^{f}
, we may write the target inequality as
T_{x}^{f}(y)\leqslant\frac{1}{2}{\lvert y-x\rvert}^{2}
,
so we begin by using the fact that
A
is pointwise symmetric to rewrite
T_{x}^{f}
in a more convenient way. We obtain

T_{x}^{f}(y)=\frac{1}{2}\left[A(y)-A(x)\right]y\cdot y+\frac{1}{2}A(x)(y-x)\cdot(y-x).

Since
A(y)-A(x)=\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathds{1}(x_{3}<0\leqslant y_{3})-\mathds{1}(y_{3}<0\leqslant x_{3})\right]e_{3}\otimes e_{3}

we compute that

T_{x}^{f}(y)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &-\frac{y_{3}^{2}}{4}+\frac{1}{2}A(x)(y-x)\cdot(y-x)&&\text{if }y_{3}<0\leqslant x_{3},\\
&\frac{x_{3}^{2}}{4}+\frac{1}{2}A(x)(y-x)\cdot(y-x)&&\text{if }x_{3}<0\leqslant y_{3},\text{ and }\\
&\frac{1}{2}A(x)(y-x)\cdot(y-x)&&\text{otherwise.}\end{aligned}\right.

Only one of these three cases is troublesome since, if
y_{3}<0\leqslant x_{3}
or if
x_{3}
and
y_{3}
have the same sign then

T_{x}^{f}(y)\leqslant\frac{1}{2}A(x)(y-x)\cdot(y-x)\leqslant\frac{1}{2}{\lvert y-x\rvert}^{2}.

In the troublesome case, i.e. if
x_{3}<0\leqslant y_{3}
, then we need to be more careful.
In this case writing
z_{h}=\left(z_{1},\,z_{2}\right)
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
tells us that

\displaystyle T_{x}^{f}(y)=\frac{1}{2}A(x)(y-x)\cdot(y-x)+\frac{1}{4}x_{3}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}{\lvert x_{h}-y_{h}\rvert}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}{(x_{3}-y_{3})}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}x_{3}^{2}.

Crucially: if
x_{3}<0\leqslant y_{3}
then
x_{3}y_{3}\geqslant 0
and hence

\frac{1}{4}{(x_{3}-y_{3})}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}x_{3}^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}{(x_{3}-y_{3})}^{2},

which proves that
T_{x}^{f}(y)\leqslant\frac{1}{2}{\lvert x-y\rvert}^{2}
even in this case, concluding the proof.
∎

With
Lemma
6.11
in hand we may now use the fact that quadratic upper bounds on
quadratic remainders

behave nicely under transformations (see
Lemmas
A.16
–
A.18
) to deduce that the true variational energy density of interest
also has a quadratic upper bound.

Lemma 6.12

(Quadratic upper bound for our variational energy density)
.

Consider
e:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by
e(x)\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{2}{\lvert x\rvert}^{2}-\frac{1}{4}{\min(r-x_{3},\,0)}^{2}
, where
r
is fixed.
For every
x,\,y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
e(y)\leqslant e(x)+De(x)(y-x)+\frac{1}{2}{\lvert x-y\rvert}^{2}
.

Proof.

We note that, for
f
as in
Lemma
6.11
,
f(ru_{3}-x)=e(x)+a(x)
for an affine function
a(x)=-rx_{3}+\frac{1}{2}r^{2}
.
Introducing the affine transformation
\Phi(x)=me_{3}-x
this means that
e=f\circ\Phi-a
.
Therefore we may use
Lemmas
A.16
,
A.17
, and
A.18
to see that
the
quadratic remainder
of
e
is given by

T^{e}_{x}=T_{x}^{f\circ\Phi}-T_{x}^{a}=T^{f}_{\Phi(x)}\circ\Phi.

In particular we deduce from
Lemma
6.11
that

T_{x}^{e}(y)\leqslant\frac{1}{2}{\lvert\Phi(y)-\Phi(x)\rvert}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}{\lvert y-x\rvert}^{2},

which is precisely the desired inequality.
∎

Our search for a quadratic upper bound on the variational energy now concludes as we leverage the quadratic upper bound on the variational energy density obtained in
Lemma
6.12

to deduce that the variational energy itself does indeed have a comparable quadratic upper bound.

Lemma 6.13

(Quadratic upper model for our variational energy)
.

Let
E_{var}
be the variational energy introduced in
Theorem
6.1
. For every
p,\,r\in\mathring{H}^{1}
, the following inequality holds:

E_{var}(r)\leqslant E_{var}(p)+DE_{var}(p)(r-p)+\frac{1}{2}{\left\lvert\left\lvert r-p\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{\mathring{H}^{1}}^{2}.

Proof.

We write
E_{var}
as in (
6.2
), abusively writing
e_{M}(\nabla p)
to mean
e_{M}(x,\,\nabla p(x))
in the sequel,
and note that item 3 of
Theorem
6.2
tells us that

DE_{var}(p)\phi=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\nabla_{u}e_{M}(\nabla p)\cdot\nabla\phi+\langle PV,\,\phi\rangle.

(6.10)

Similarly we may reinterpret
Lemma
6.12
as telling us that

e_{M}(\nabla r)\leqslant e_{M}(\nabla p)+\nabla_{u}e_{M}(\nabla p)\cdot\nabla(r-p)+\frac{1}{2}{\lvert\nabla(r-p)\rvert}^{2}.

(6.11)

Putting (
6.2
), (
6.10
), and (
6.11
) together then yields the claim.
∎

We now have all of the ingredients we need to prove the convergence rates for our three descent methods in the context of
exact
line search.
First we state a generic version of that result in
Proposition
6.14

before specialising it to our energy afterwards in
Theorem
6.16
.
Note that the result below is classical – see for example
[
BV04
]
. We record it here, along with its proof, in order to ensure that
good care is taken of tracking the constants arising from comparable norms involved in the descent method.
This is particularly important for us since ultimately it will lead to significant differences between the convergence rate of the
L^{2}
-gradient descent compared with the other descent methods.

Proposition 6.14

(Key inequality for the convergence rate of gradient descent with exact line search in Hilbert spaces)
.

Let
{\left(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\right)}_{X}
be a complete inner product on a Hilbert space
H
for which there exists a constant
C>0
such that

{\left\lvert\left\lvert u\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{H}\leqslant C{\left\lvert\left\lvert u\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{X}\text{ for every }u\in H.

(6.12)

Let
\mathcal{F}:H\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuously Fréchet differentiable functional satisfying, for some constants
m,\,M>0
,

\frac{m}{2}{\left\lvert\left\lvert v-u\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{H}^{2}\leqslant\mathcal{F}(v)-\mathcal{F}(u)-D\mathcal{F}(u)(v-u)\leqslant\frac{M}{2}{\left\lvert\left\lvert v-u\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{H}^{2}

(6.13)

for every
u,\,v\in H
, and let
u^{*}
denote the unique minimizer of
\mathcal{F}
.
Then

\mathcal{F}\left(u-\frac{1}{C^{2}M}\nabla_{X}\mathcal{F}(u)\right)\leqslant\mathcal{F}(u)-\frac{1}{2C^{2}M}{\left\lvert\left\lvert\nabla_{X}\mathcal{F}(u)\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{X}^{2}

(6.14)

and

\mathcal{F}(u)-\mathcal{F}(u^{*})\leqslant\frac{1}{2m}{\left\lvert\left\lvert\nabla_{H}\mathcal{F}(u)\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{H}^{2}

(6.15)

such that

\mathcal{F}\left(u-\frac{1}{C^{2}M}\nabla_{X}\mathcal{F}(u)\right)-\mathcal{F}(u^{*})\leqslant\left(1-\frac{m}{C^{4}M}\right)\left(\mathcal{F}(u)-\mathcal{F}(u^{*})\right).

(6.16)

Remark 6.15

(On the importance of the key inequality)
.

The so-called “key inequality” (
6.16
) warrants such a lofty name since the actual convergence rate follows from that inequality immediately.
Indeed, if we denote let
x_{0}\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
and denote by
x_{k}
the iterates obtained via gradient descent with exact line search, i.e.

x_{k}\vcentcolon=\argmin_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{F}\left(x_{k-1}-t\nabla\mathcal{F}(x_{k-1})\right),

then inducting on the key inequality tells us that

\mathcal{F}(x_{k-1})-\mathcal{F}(x^{*})\leqslant{\left(1-\frac{m}{C^{4}M}\right)}^{k}\left(\mathcal{F}(x_{0})-\mathcal{F}(x^{*})\right),

which guarantees that indeed
\mathcal{F}(x_{k})
converges to
\mathcal{F}(x_{0})
.

Proof of
Proposition
6.14
.

Plugging
v=u-t\nabla_{X}\mathcal{F}(u)
into the second inequality in (
6.13
) tells us that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\left(u-t\nabla_{X}\mathcal{F}(u)\right)\leqslant\mathcal{F}(u)-t{\left\lvert\left\lvert\nabla_{X}\mathcal{F}(u)\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{X}^{2}+\frac{Mt^{2}}{2}{\left\lvert\left\lvert\nabla_{X}\mathcal{F}(u)\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathcal{F}(u)+\left(\frac{C^{2}Mt^{2}}{2}-t\right){\left\lvert\left\lvert\nabla_{X}\mathcal{F}(u)\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{X}^{2}.

Since the quadratic
q(t)\vcentcolon=\frac{C^{2}Mt^{2}}{2}-t
is minimized at
t^{*}=\frac{1}{C^{2}M}
, where it attains the value
q(t^{*})=-\frac{1}{2C^{2}M}
,
we deduce (
6.14
) from the inequality above.

To establish (
6.15
) we use the first inequality in (
6.13
).
We begin by writing
D\mathcal{F}(u)(v-u)={\left(\nabla_{H}\mathcal{F}(u),\,v-u\right)}_{H}
, which tells us that with respect to
v
the right-hand side of that inequality
is minimized at
v^{*}=u-\frac{1}{m}\nabla_{H}\mathcal{F}(u)
.
Therefore, for any
u,\,v\in H
,

F(v)\geqslant\left[\mathcal{F}(u)+{\left(\nabla_{H}\mathcal{F}(u),\,v-u\right)}_{H}+\frac{m}{2}{\left\lvert\left\lvert v-u\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{H}^{2}\right]|_{v=v^{*}}=\mathcal{F}(u)-\frac{1}{2m}{\left\lvert\left\lvert\nabla_{H}\mathcal{F}(u)\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{H}^{2}.

Setting
v=u^{*}
produces (
6.15
).

Finally we turn our attention to the key inequality (
6.16
).
(
6.12
) and (
6.15
) tell us that

{\left\lvert\left\lvert\nabla_{X}\mathcal{F}(u)\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{X}^{2}\geqslant\frac{2m}{C^{2}}\left(\mathcal{F}(u)-\mathcal{F}(u^{*})\right)

and so combining this inequality with (
6.14
) yields (
6.16
).
∎

We now specialise the generic result proved above to the case of our variational energy.

Theorem 6.16

(Convergence rates for our variational energy with exact line search)
.

Let
E_{var}
be the variational energy introduced in
Theorem
6.1
,
let
V
be a non-empty subspace of
\mathring{H}^{1}
(possibly equal to
\mathring{H}^{1}
),
and let
p^{*}
be the unique minimizer of
E_{var}
in
V
.

1.

Let
p\in V
and let
\pi=-\nabla_{\mathring{H}^{1}}E_{var}|_{V}(p)
denote the
\mathring{H}^{1}
-gradient
descent direction
for
E_{var}|_{V}
at
p
. Then

E_{var}(p+\pi)-E_{var}(p^{*})\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\left(E_{var}(p)-E_{var}(p^{*})\right).

2.

Let
p\in V
and let
\pi
denote the
Newton descent direction
for
E_{var}|_{V}
at
p
. Then

E_{var}\left(p+\frac{1}{4}\pi\right)-E_{var}(p^{*})\leqslant\frac{31}{32}\left(E_{var}(p)-E_{var}(p^{*})\right).

3.

Suppose that
V\subseteq H^{2}
, suppose that there exist a constant
C_{n}>0
such that

{\left\lvert\left\lvert p\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{\mathring{H}^{1}}\leqslant C_{n}{\left\lvert\left\lvert p\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{L^{2}}

for every
p\in V
, and suppose that
M\in H^{1}
and
PV\in L^{2}
.
Let
p\in V
and let
\pi=-\nabla_{L^{2}}E_{var}|_{V}(p)
denote the
L^{2}
-gradient descent direction for
E_{var}|_{V}
at
p
. Then

E_{var}\left(p+\frac{1}{C_{n}^{2}}\pi\right)-E_{var}(p^{*})\leqslant\left(1-\frac{1}{2C_{n}^{4}}\right)\left(E_{var}(p)-E_{var}(p^{*})\right).

Proof.

Since all of these descent directions are gradient descents, we need to verify that the hypotheses of
Proposition
6.14
are verified.
Item 4 of
Theorem
6.2
guarantees that
E_{var}
is continuously Fréchet differentiable,
item 1 of
Theorem
6.2
,
Lemma
6.13
, and
Lemma
A.15
show that (
6.13
)
is verified with
m=\frac{1}{2}
and
M=1
, and
Theorem
6.3
verifies that
E_{var}
does indeed have a unique minimizer.
The only hypothesis left to check is (
6.12
),
and the value of the constant
C
varies from case to case since the inner product
{\left(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\right)}_{X}
used to define the gradient differs in each case.

1.

In this case
{\left(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\right)}_{X}={\left(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\right)}_{\mathring{H}^{1}}
and so
C=1
.

2.

In this case we may use the
Hessian inner product
recorded in
Proposition
6.8
to see that

{\left\lvert\left\lvert\phi\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{D^{2}_{p}E_{var}}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}{\lvert\nabla\phi\rvert}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\mathds{1}(M<\partial_{3}p)\geqslant\frac{1}{2}{\left\lvert\left\lvert\phi\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{\mathring{H}^{1}}^{2},

i.e.
C=2
.

3.

In this case
C=C_{n}
, by assumption.

∎

Remark 6.17

(On the convergence rate of
L^{2}
-gradient descent)
.

We comment on the constant
C_{n}
which appears in the third item of
Theorem
6.16
above.
Note that in practice such a constant exists since we work with
V
a finite-dimensional subspace of
\mathring{H}^{1}
, in which case all norms on
V
are equivalent.
Moreover, say that in such an instance the dimension of
V
is given by
n
(typically commensurate with the number of elements in a finite-element scheme).
Then, as
n
approaches infinity, the constant
C_{n}
would necessarily grow unboundedly, which would in turn mean that

1-\frac{1}{2C_{n}^{4}}\uparrow 1
.
This means that in the case of
L^{2}
-gradient descent the convergence rate remains linear but the constant involved worsens (i.e. approaches one) as the dimension of the subspace
we work with increases (e.g. as the mesh is refined).

Note that this remark also apply when backtracking is used instead of exact line search – c.f.
Theorem
6.20
below.

We now turn our attention towards the second, and arguably more important, set of convergence rates recorded in this section: those arising from
backtracking
line search.
For we define backtracking line search below. Recall that, by contrast with exact line search discussed so far, backtracking line search can be implemented immediately in practical situations
(which is typically not the case for exact line search) while retaining enough favourable structure such that provable convergence rates are still in play with backtracking line search.

Definition 6.18

(Backtracking line search)
.

Consider a functional
\mathcal{F}:B\to\mathbb{R}
on a Banach space
B
, let
x\in B
be a current iterate, let
\Delta x\in B
be a search direction,
and consider two parameters
\alpha\in(0,\,1/2)
and
\beta\in(0,\,1)
.
Backtracking line search proceeds as follows: initiate the step size
t_{0}=1
and, for
i\geqslant 0
, if

f\left(x+t_{i}\Delta x\right)>f(x)+\alpha tDf(x)\Delta x

then set
t_{i+1}\vcentcolon=\beta t_{i}
, while if

f\left(x+t_{i}\Delta x\right)\leqslant f(x)+\alpha tDf(x)\Delta x

(6.17)

then set the step size
t_{*}\vcentcolon=t_{i}
and terminate.

As we did above in the context of exact line search when specialising from the generic result of
Proposition
6.14

to
Theorem
6.16
which applies to the variational energy of interest,
we first record a generic result for the convergence rate of gradient descent methods with
backtracking
line search.
As with
Proposition
6.14
above, such a result is classical (see
[
BV04
]
)
and its proof is provided here in order to carefully track the
constants that appear due to the presence of comparable norms.

Proposition 6.19

(Key inequality for the convergence rate of gradient descent with exact line search in Hilbert spaces)
.

Consider the same notation and assumptions as
Proposition
6.14
and let
\alpha\in(0,\,1/2)
and
\beta\in(0,\,1)
.
For any
u\in H
and any
t\in\left[0,\,\frac{1}{C^{2}M}\right]
,

\mathcal{F}\left(u-t\nabla_{X}\mathcal{F}(u)\right)\leqslant\mathcal{F}(u)-\alpha t{\left\lvert\left\lvert\nabla_{X}\mathcal{F}(u)\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{X}^{2},

(6.18)

i.e. the
backtracking
exit condition (
6.17
) holds.
Moreover, for any
u\in H
if
t_{*}
denotes the step size obtained via backtracking line search with parameters
\alpha
and
\beta
and search direction
-\nabla_{X}\mathcal{F}(u)

then

t_{*}\geqslant\min\left(1,\,\frac{\beta}{C^{2}M}\right)

(6.19)

and

\mathcal{F}\left(u-t_{*}\nabla_{X}\mathcal{F}(u)\right)-\mathcal{F}(u^{*})\leqslant\left(1-\frac{2\alpha m}{C^{2}}\min\left(1,\,\frac{\beta}{C^{2}M}\right)\right)\left(\mathcal{F}(u)-\mathcal{F}(u^{*})\right).

Proof.

Proceeding as in the proof of
Proposition
6.14
we deduce from (
6.13
) that

\mathcal{F}\left(u-t\nabla_{X}\mathcal{F}(u)\right)\leqslant\mathcal{F}(u)+\left(\frac{C^{2}Mt^{2}}{2}-t\right){\left\lvert\left\lvert\nabla_{X}\mathcal{F}(u)\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{X}^{2}.

In particular we note that if
t\in\left[0,\,\frac{1}{C^{2}M}\right]
then
\frac{C^{2}Mt^{2}}{2}-t\leqslant-\frac{t}{2}<-\alpha t
,
and so indeed (
6.18
) holds.
As a consequence this means that either
t_{*}=t_{0}=1
or
t_{*}=\beta t_{i}
for some
t_{i}
which did
not
satisfy the backtracking exit condition,
and so which must satisfy
t_{i}>\frac{1}{C^{2}M}
. Putting these two options together establishes (
6.19
).
Finally we note that
t_{*}
must satisfy the backtracking exit condition (
6.18
)
and so combining that inequality with (
6.13
), (
6.15
),
and (
6.19
) allows us to obtain the main inequality.
∎

We now specialise
Proposition
6.19
above to our variational energy.

Theorem 6.20

(Convergence rates for our variational energy with backtracking line search)
.

Let
E_{var}
be the variational energy introduced in
Theorem
6.1
,
let
V
be a non-empty subspace of
\mathring{H}^{1}
(possibly equal to
\mathring{H}^{1}
),
let
p^{*}
denote the unique minimizer of
E_{var}
in
V
, and let
\alpha\in(0,\,1/2)
and
\beta\in(0,\,1)
.
In each case below let
t_{*}
denote the step size obtained via
backtracking
line search with direction
\pi

(chosen differently in each case) and parameters
\alpha
and
\beta
.

1.

Let
p\in V
and let
\pi=-\nabla_{\mathring{H}^{1}}E_{var}|_{V}(p)
denote the

\mathring{H}^{1}
-gradient
descent direction
for
E_{var}|_{V}
at
p
. Then

t_{*}\geqslant\beta\text{ and }E_{var}(p+t_{*}\pi)-E_{var}(p^{*})\leqslant(1-\alpha\beta)\left(E_{var}(p)-E_{var}(p^{*})\right).

2.

Let
p\in V
and let
\pi
denote the
Newton descent direction
for
E_{var}|_{V}
at
p
.
Then

t_{*}\geqslant\frac{\beta}{4}\text{ and }E_{var}(p+t_{*}\pi)-E_{var}(p^{*})\leqslant\left(1-\frac{\alpha\beta}{16}\right)\left(E_{var}(p)-E_{var}(p^{*})\right).

3.

Suppose that
V\subseteq H^{2}
, suppose that there exist a constant
C_{n}>0
such that

{\left\lvert\left\lvert p\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{\mathring{H}^{1}}\leqslant C_{n}{\left\lvert\left\lvert p\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{L^{2}}

for every
p\in V
, and suppose that
M\in H^{1}
and
PV\in L^{2}
.
Let
p\in V
and let
\pi=-\nabla_{L^{2}}E_{var}|_{V}(p)
denote the
L^{2}
-gradient descent direction for
E_{var}|_{V}
at
p
.
Then

t_{*}\geqslant\min\left(1,\,\frac{\beta}{C_{n}^{2}}\right)

and

E_{var}(p+t_{*}\pi)-E_{var}(p^{*})\leqslant\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{C_{n}^{2}}\min\left(1,\,\frac{\beta}{C_{n}^{2}}\right)\right)\left(E_{var}(p)-E_{var}(p^{*})\right).

Proof.

This follows from
Proposition
6.19

just as
Theorem
6.16
follows from
Proposition
6.14
,
noting that the appropriate values of the constant
C
in each case were identified in the proof of
Theorem
6.16
.
∎

With the convergence rates in hand we change gear and turn our attention towards a consistency result.
We seek to prove that minimizers of the variational energy restricted to (appropriate) finite-dimensional subspaces of
\mathring{H}^{1}
do converge to the true minimizer.
In order to do so we first prove the following elementary estimate.

Lemma 6.21

(Explicit local modulus of continuity of the variational energy)
.

Let
E_{var}
be the variational energy introduced in
Theorem
6.1
.
For any
p_{1},\,p_{2}\in\mathring{H}^{1}
the following estimate holds.

E_{var}(p_{1})-E_{var}(p_{2})\leqslant\left(\frac{1}{2}{\left\lvert\left\lvert M\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{L^{2}}+\frac{3}{4}{\left\lvert\left\lvert\nabla p_{1}\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{L^{2}}+\frac{3}{4}{\left\lvert\left\lvert\nabla p_{2}\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{L^{2}}\right){\left\lvert\left\lvert\nabla(p_{1}-p_{2})\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{L^{2}}.

(6.20)

Proof.

We write
g_{0}(s)\vcentcolon=\min(s,\,0)
such that

E_{var}(p_{1})-E_{var}(p_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\frac{1}{2}\left({\lvert\nabla p_{1}\rvert}^{2}-{\lvert\nabla p_{2}\rvert}^{2}\right)-\frac{1}{4}\left[g_{0}(M-\partial_{3}p_{1})-g_{0}(M-\partial_{3}p_{2})\right].

We then estimate

\left|{\lvert\nabla p_{1}\rvert}^{2}-{\lvert\nabla p_{2}\rvert}^{2}\right\rvert=\left|\nabla(p_{1}+p_{2})\right\rvert\;\left|\nabla(p_{1}-p_{2})\right\rvert\leqslant\left(\lvert\nabla p_{1}\rvert+\lvert\nabla p_{2}\rvert\right)\lvert\nabla(p_{1}-p_{2})\rvert

and, since
g_{0}
is
1
-Lipschitz, we may proceed in the same way to estimate

\left|{g_{0}(M-\partial_{3}p_{1})}^{2}-{g_{0}(M-\partial_{3}p_{2})}^{2}\right\rvert\leqslant\left(2\lvert M\rvert+\lvert\partial_{3}p_{1}\rvert+\lvert\partial_{3}p_{2}\rvert\right)\lvert\partial_{3}(p_{1}-p_{2})\rvert.

Putting it all together produces the desired inequality.
∎

We are now equipped to prove the last main result of this section, which pertains to numerical consistency.

Theorem 6.22

(Convergence of the minimizers of the approximate variational energy to the minimizer of the true variational energy)
.

Let
V_{n}\subseteq\mathring{H}^{1}
be a closed subspace of
\mathring{H}^{1}
, define
E_{n}\vcentcolon=E_{var}|_{V_{n}}
, and let

p^{*}\vcentcolon=\argmin E_{var}\text{ and }p^{*}_{n}\vcentcolon=\argmin E_{n}.

For any
p^{\varepsilon}_{n},\,p_{n}\in V_{n}
,

\displaystyle{\left\lvert\left\lvert p_{n}^{\varepsilon}-p^{*}\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{\mathring{H}^{1}}

(6.21)

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\left[E_{n}(p_{n}^{\varepsilon})-\min E_{n}\right]+\left(8{\left\lvert\left\lvert M\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{L^{2}}+12{\left\lvert\left\lvert PV\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{H^{-1}}+3{\left\lvert\left\lvert p_{n}-p^{*}\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{\mathring{H}^{1}}\right){\left\lvert\left\lvert p_{n}-p^{*}\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{\mathring{H}^{1}}.

In particular, if
p^{\varepsilon}_{n}
and
p_{n}
are chosen to satisfy, for some
\alpha,\,C>0
,

E_{n}(p_{n}^{\varepsilon})-\min E_{n}\leqslant\varepsilon\text{ and }{\left\lvert\left\lvert p_{n}-p^{*}\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{\mathring{H}^{1}}\leqslant Cn^{-\alpha}\text{ for all }n

(6.22)

then, for
n
sufficiently large to ensure
Cn^{-\alpha}\leqslant 1
,

{\left\lvert\left\lvert p_{n}^{\varepsilon}-p^{*}\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{\mathring{H}^{1}}\lesssim\varepsilon+\left(1+{\left\lvert\left\lvert M\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{L^{2}}+{\left\lvert\left\lvert PV\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{H^{-}1}\right)n^{-\alpha},

(6.23)

i.e.
\varepsilon
-minimizers of the discretised variational energy
E_{n}
can be made arbitrarily close to the minimizer of the true variational energy
E_{var}
.

Proof.

First note that the
p_{n}^{*}
’s are well-defined since the properties of
E_{var}
parlay over to properties of
E_{n}
and the proof of
Theorem
6.3

can be reproduced for
E_{n}
.

Since (
6.23
) follows from (
6.21
)
under the additional assumptions of (
6.22
) it suffices to establish (
6.21
).
First we note that, since
p^{*}
and
p^{*}_{n}
are minimizers of
E_{var}
and
E_{n}
, respectively, item 5 of
Theorem
6.2

(which holds
mutatis mutandis
for
E_{n}
) tells us that

\displaystyle{\left\lvert\left\lvert p_{n}^{\varepsilon}-p^{*}\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{\mathring{H}^{1}}\leqslant{\left\lvert\left\lvert p_{n}^{\varepsilon}-p_{n}^{*}\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{\mathring{H}^{1}}+{\left\lvert\left\lvert p_{n}^{*}-p^{*}\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{\mathring{H}^{1}}

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\left[E_{n}(p_{n}^{\varepsilon})-E_{n}(p_{n}^{*})\right]+4\left[E_{var}(p_{n}^{*})-E_{var}(p^{*})\right].

(6.24)

By virtue of
Theorem
6.3
and
6.21
we may estimate the second term above via

\displaystyle E_{var}(p_{n}^{*})-E_{var}(p^{*})

\displaystyle=E_{n}(p_{n}^{*})-E_{var}(p^{*})=\min E_{n}-E_{var}(p^{*})\leqslant E_{var}(p_{n})-E_{var}(p^{*})

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\frac{1}{2}{\left\lvert\left\lvert M\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{L^{2}}+\frac{3}{4}\left[{\left\lvert\left\lvert p_{n}\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{\mathring{H}^{1}}+{\left\lvert\left\lvert p^{*}\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{\mathring{H}^{1}}\right]\right){\left\lvert\left\lvert p_{n}-p^{*}\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{\mathring{H}^{1}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(2{\left\lvert\left\lvert M\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{L^{2}}+3{\left\lvert\left\lvert PV\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{H^{-1}}+\frac{3}{4}{\left\lvert\left\lvert p_{n}-p^{*}\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{\mathring{H}^{1}}\right){\left\lvert\left\lvert p_{n}-p^{*}\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{\mathring{H}^{1}}.

(6.25)

Putting (
6.24
) and (
6.25
)
together then produces (
6.21
).
∎

6.2
Geometry

In this section we do two things.

•

Foreshadowed by
Proposition
3.1
we see that
p
and
M
may be viewed as
coordinates
along the balanced set
\mathcal{B}
.
Moreover
PV
and
M
then arise naturally as
dual coordinates
(with respect to some natural inner product arising from the energy).

•

Equipped with this geometric language we interpret the Newton descent iteration of
Section
6.1
above in geometric terms.
We explain how the Newton iteration can be viewed as a flow along the balanced set
\mathcal{B}
.
This flow follows the coordinate direction along
M
while driving
PV
towards its true value.

We begin by recalling a definition from
Section
3
.

Definition 6.23

(Global chart for the balanced set)
.

For
\mathbb{H}\vcentcolon=\mathring{H}^{1}\times L^{2}
we define
\Phi:\mathbb{H}\to\mathcal{B}
via

\Phi(p,\,M)=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{h}^{\perp}p\\
\partial_{3}p+\frac{1}{2}{\min}_{0}\,(M-\partial_{3}p)\\
M-\partial_{3}p-\frac{1}{2}{\min}_{0}\,(M-\partial_{3}p)\end{pmatrix}.

Figure 7
:
A depiction of the global chart for the balanced set introduced in
Definition
6.23
.

We now collect some more properties of this map.

Proposition 6.24
.

\Phi
is bi–Lipschitz, i.e. Lipschitz with Lipschitz inverse.

In particular, since the parametrisation
\Phi
of
\mathcal{B}
is bi-Lipschitz it follows that the balanced set is an honest-to-goodness manifold. This is depicted in
Figure
7
.

Corollary 6.25
.

The balanced set
\mathcal{B}
is a Lipschitz-regular Hilbert manifold, modeled after the Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}
, with global chart
\Phi
.

Proof of
Corollary
6.25
.

This follows from immediately from
Proposition
6.24
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition
6.24
.

First we note that the inverse of
\Phi
is given by

\Phi^{-1}(u,\,\theta,\,q)=\begin{pmatrix}\Delta^{-1}\left[\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\cdot u_{h}+\partial_{3}(\theta-{\min}_{0}\,q)\right]\\
\theta+q\end{pmatrix}.

Indeed, suppose that
(u,\,\theta,\,q)=\Phi(p,\,M)
.
We see that

\sign q=\sign(M-\partial_{3}p)

and so
Lemmas
A.6
and
A.5
tell us that

\displaystyle(u,\,\theta,\,q)=\Phi(p,\,M)

\displaystyle\iff\left\{\begin{aligned} &u=\nabla_{h}^{\perp}p\\
&\theta=\partial_{3}p+\frac{1}{2}{\min}_{0}\,(M-\partial_{3}p)\\
&q=M-\partial_{3}p-\frac{1}{2}{\min}_{0}\,(M-\partial_{3}p)\end{aligned}\right.

\displaystyle\iff\left\{\begin{aligned} &u=\nabla_{h}^{\perp}p\\
&\theta+q=M\\
&\theta-{\min}_{0}\,q=\partial_{3}p\end{aligned}\right.

\displaystyle\iff\left\{\begin{aligned} &-u_{h}^{\perp}+(\theta-{\min}_{0}\,q)e_{3}=\nabla p\\
&u_{3}=0\\
&\theta+q=M\end{aligned}\right.

\displaystyle\iff\left\{\begin{aligned} &p=\Delta^{-1}\nabla\cdot\left[-u_{h}^{\perp}+(\theta-{\min}_{0}\,q)e_{3}\right]\\
&u_{3}=0\\
&M=\theta+q\end{aligned}\right.

\displaystyle\iff\left\{\begin{aligned} &p=\Delta^{-1}\left[\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\cdot u_{h}+\partial_{3}(\theta-{\min}_{0}\,q)\right]\\
&M=\theta+q\\
&u_{3}=0\end{aligned}\right.

\displaystyle\iff(p,\,M)=\Phi^{-1}(u,\,\theta,\,q).

The fact that both
\Phi
and
\Phi^{-1}
are Lipschitz then follows from the fact that
s\mapsto{\min}_{0}\,s
is Lipschitz, that
\Delta:\mathring{H}^{1}\to H^{-1}
is an isometry,
and from
Lemma
A.14
.
Indeed, for
\Phi
we have that

\displaystyle{\left\lvert\left\lvert\Phi(p_{1},\,M_{1})-\Phi(p_{2},\,M_{2})\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim{\left\lvert\left\lvert\nabla p_{1}-\nabla p_{2}\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{L^{2}}^{2}+{\left\lvert\left\lvert M_{1}-M_{2}\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle={\left\lvert\left\lvert(p_{1},\,M_{1})-(p_{2},\,M_{2})\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{\mathring{H}^{1}\times L^{2}}^{2}

while for
\Phi^{-1}
we have that

\displaystyle{\left\lvert\left\lvert\Phi^{-1}(u_{1},\,\theta_{1},\,q_{1})-\Phi^{-1}(u_{2},\,\theta_{2},\,q_{2})\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{\mathring{H}^{1}\times L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle={\left\lvert\left\lvert\Delta^{-1}\left[\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\cdot u_{h,\,1}+\partial_{3}(\theta_{1}-{\min}_{0}\,q_{1})-\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\cdot u_{h,\,2}-\partial_{3}(\theta_{2}-{\min}_{0}\,q_{2})\right]\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{\mathring{H}^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+{\left\lvert\left\lvert(\theta_{1}+q_{1})-(\theta_{2}+q_{2})\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle={\left\lvert\left\lvert\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\cdot u_{h,\,1}+\partial_{3}(\theta_{1}-{\min}_{0}\,q_{1})-\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\cdot u_{h,\,2}-\partial_{3}(\theta_{2}-{\min}_{0}\,q_{2})\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{H^{-1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+{\left\lvert\left\lvert(\theta_{1}+q_{1})-(\theta_{2}+q_{2})\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim{\left\lvert\left\lvert u_{h,\,1}-u_{h,\,2}\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{L^{2}}^{2}+{\left\lvert\left\lvert(\theta_{1}-{\min}_{0}\,q_{1})-(\theta_{2}-{\min}_{0}\,q_{2})\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{L^{2}}^{2}+{\left\lvert\left\lvert(\theta_{1}+q_{1})-(\theta_{2}+q_{2})\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim{\left\lvert\left\lvert(u_{1},\,\theta_{1},\,q_{1})-(u_{2},\,\theta_{2},\,q_{2})\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{L^{2}}^{2}.\qed

Remark 6.26

(Alternate form of
\Phi^{-1}
)
.

We typically write
\Phi^{-1}
in terms of the nonlinear
PV
-and-
M
inversion,
and not in terms of the inversion of a Laplacian as is done in the proof of
Proposition
6.24
above.
This is because the Laplacian inversion does not use
PV
, but instead uses a potential vorticity based on the
buoyancy

(by contrast with
PV
which is based on the equivalent potential temperature
\theta
) given by

{PV}_{b}=\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\cdot u_{h}+\partial_{3}(\theta-{\min}_{0}\,q).

Typically we do
not
want to use
{PV}_{b}
since it is not slow. That is why
PV
is usually preferable.
However, for the purpose of the proof of
Proposition
6.24
,
{PV}_{b}
is preferable to
PV

since it lets us prove more easily that
\Phi^{-1}
is Lipschitz.

So far we have been able to endow the balanced set
\mathcal{B}
with the structure of a Lipschitz-regular manifold.
Unfortunately, that is as regular as the balanced set gets due to the presence of
{\min}_{0}\,q
in the buoyancy term,
which is Lipschitz but not differentiable.

Nonetheless we can formally differentiate
{\min}_{0}\,q
and thus endow the balanced set
\mathcal{B}
with a formal differentiable structure.
In order to do so, we introduce notation for its formal tangent space, for the formal derivative of the chart
\Phi
, and for the inner product we will use (which comes from the conserved energy).

Definition 6.27
.

Fix an indicator function
H
.

1.

We define

\displaystyle T_{H}\mathcal{B}\vcentcolon=\bigg\{(u,\,\theta,\,q)\in\mathbb{L}^{2}_{\sigma}:

\displaystyle\;\partial_{3}u_{h}-\nabla_{h}^{\perp}(\theta-qH)=0,\,u_{3}=0,\,

\displaystyle\fint u_{h}=0,\text{ and }\fint(\theta-qH)=0\bigg\}.

2.

We define
D_{H}\Phi:\mathbb{H}\to T_{H}\mathcal{B}
via

D_{H}\Phi(p,\,M)\vcentcolon=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{h}^{\perp}p\\
\partial_{3}p+\frac{H}{2}(M-\partial_{3}p)\\
M-\partial_{3}p-\frac{H}{2}(M-\partial_{3}p)\end{pmatrix}.

3.

For any
(u,\,\theta,q)\in\mathbb{L}^{2}_{\sigma}
we define

{\left\lvert\left\lvert(u,\,\theta,q)\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{L^{2}_{H}}^{2}\vcentcolon=\int{\lvert u\rvert}^{2}+\theta^{2}+q^{2}H+{(\theta+q)}^{2}

and let
{\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle}_{L^{2}_{H}}
denote the corresponding innner product.

As expected, the formal derivative of
\Phi
completely characterizes the formal tangent space.
(This would be trivially true if the balanced set
\mathcal{B}
was endowed with an honest-to-goodness differentiable structure by its chart
\Phi
.)

Lemma 6.28
.

T_{H}\mathcal{B}=\im D_{H}\Phi
.

Proof.

Proceeding similarly to the proof of
Proposition
3.1
we introduce the “good unknown” (see
Remark
3.2
)

v_{H}\vcentcolon=-u_{h}^{\perp}+(\theta-qH)e_{3}.

For
\mathcal{X}=(u,\,\theta,\,q)
we have on one hand that, as per
Lemma
A.5
,

\mathcal{X}\in T_{H}\mathcal{B}\iff\nabla\times v_{H},\,\fint v_{H}=0,\text{ and }u_{3}=0.

On the other hand, proceeding as in the proof of
Proposition
6.24
and using
Lemma
A.6
tells us that

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}=D_{H}\Phi(p,\,M)

\displaystyle\iff\left\{\begin{aligned} &-u_{h}^{\perp}+(\theta-qH)e_{3}=\nabla p\\
&u_{3}=0\\
&\theta+q=0\end{aligned}\right.

\displaystyle\iff\left\{\begin{aligned} &v_{H}=\nabla p\\
&u_{3}=0\\
&\theta+q=M\end{aligned}\right.

The Helmholtz decomposition (
Corollary
A.2
) allows us to conclude:

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}\in T_{H}\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle\iff\nabla\times v_{H}=0,\,\fint v_{H}=0,\text{ and }u_{3}=0

\displaystyle\iff\nabla\times v_{H}=0,\,\fint v_{H}=0,\,u_{3}=0,\text{ and }\theta+q=\vcentcolon M

\displaystyle\iff v_{H}=\nabla p\text{ for some }p\in\mathring{H}^{1},\,u_{3}=0,\text{ and }\theta+q=M

\displaystyle\iff\mathcal{X}=D_{H}\Phi(p,\,M)\text{ for some }(p,\,M)\in\mathbb{H}

\displaystyle\iff\mathcal{X}\in\im D_{H}\Phi.\qed

Figure 8
:
The formal tangent space to the balanced set introduced in
Definition
6.27

is the image of the formal derivative of the global chart.

We now remark that, as suggested by the notation we chose to employ,
D_{H}\Phi
may be viewed formally as the derivative of
\Phi
.
Since the balanced set
\mathcal{B}
is the image of the chart
\Phi
, it follows that the image of its formal derivative
D_{H}\Phi

may be interpreted formally as the tangent space to that same balanced set.
Since
Lemma
6.28
above tells us that the image of
D_{H}\Phi
is precisely
T_{H}\mathcal{B}
,
we see that once again the notation was chosen to be suggestive:
T_{H}\mathcal{B}
may be viewed formally as the tangent space to the balanced set
\mathcal{B}
.
This is made precise in
Remark
6.29
below and is depicted in
Figure
8
.

Remark 6.29
.

Formally, we may compute that, for
H\vcentcolon=\mathds{1}(q<0)
and for any derivative
\partial
,

\partial({\min}_{0}\,q)=(\partial q)\mathds{1}(q<0)=(\partial q)H.

This shows that
D_{H}\Phi
is, formally, the derivative of
\Phi
.
Since
\Phi
is a global chart for the Hilbert manifold
\mathcal{B}
this means that we may formally view
T_{H}\mathcal{B}
,
which is the image of
D_{H}\Phi
, as the tangent space of
\mathcal{B}
.

An alternative justification, more direct albeit still formal, is as follows.
Let
\mathcal{X}=(u,\,\theta,\,q):(-1,\,1)\to\mathcal{B}
be a curve of balanced states
and let
\mathcal{Z}
denote its derivative at zero, i.e.

\mathcal{Z}=(v,\,\phi,\,r)\vcentcolon=\frac{d}{ds}\mathcal{X}(s)\Big|_{s=0}.

Since
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{B}
it follows that

j(\mathcal{X})=0,\,w(\mathcal{X})=0,\text{ and }a(\mathcal{X})=0.

Both
w
and
a
are linear, so we deduce immediately that

w(\mathcal{Z})=0\text{ and }a(\mathcal{Z})=0.

What about
j
? Using the formal identity
\partial({\min}_{0}\,q)=(\partial q)H
discussed above we compute that

0=\frac{d}{ds}j(\mathcal{X})\Big|_{s=0}=\frac{d}{ds}\left[\partial_{3}u_{h}-\nabla_{h}^{\perp}(\theta-{\min}_{0}\,q)\right]\Big|_{s=0}=\partial_{3}v_{h}-\nabla_{h}^{\perp}(\phi-rH)=\vcentcolon j_{H}(\mathcal{Z}).

This means that
\mathcal{Z}
belongs to
T_{H}\mathcal{B}
.
In other words: if
\mathcal{X}
is a curve of balanced states then its formal tangent vector
\mathcal{Z}
lies in
T_{H}\mathcal{B}
.
This warrants treating formally
T_{H}\mathcal{B}
as the tangent space of the balanced set
\mathcal{B}
.

Note that since the chart
\Phi
is invertible and since
D_{H}\Phi
is its formal derivative,
we may expect
D_{H}\Phi
to inherit this invertibility. This is indeed the case.
We record this result here since it will be of use later.

Lemma 6.30
.

D_{H}\Phi
is invertible.

Proof.

This follows as in the proof that
\Phi
is invertible (
Proposition
6.24
) since

(1+H)(1-H/2)=1
and so

\displaystyle(u,\,\theta,\,q)=D_{H}\Phi(p,\,M)

\displaystyle\iff\left\{\begin{aligned} &u=\nabla_{h}^{\perp}p\\
&\theta=\partial_{3}p+\frac{H}{2}(M-\partial_{3}p)\\
&q=M-\partial_{3}p-\frac{H}{2}(M-\partial_{3}p)\end{aligned}\right.

\displaystyle\iff\left\{\begin{aligned} &-u_{h}^{\perp}+(\theta-qH)e_{3}=\nabla p\\
&u_{3}=0\\
&\theta+q=M\end{aligned}\right.

\displaystyle\iff\left\{\begin{aligned} &p=\Delta^{-1}\nabla\cdot\left[-u_{h}^{\perp}+(\theta-qH)e_{3}\right]\\
&u_{3}=0\\
&M=\theta+q\end{aligned}\right.

\displaystyle\iff\left\{\begin{aligned} &p=\Delta^{-1}\left[\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\cdot u_{h}+\partial_{3}(\theta-qH)\right]\\
&u_{3}=0\\
&M=\theta+q\end{aligned}\right.

and so the inverse is given by

{(D_{H}\Phi)}^{-1}(u,\,\theta,\,q)=\begin{pmatrix}\Delta^{-1}\left[\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\cdot u_{h}+\partial_{3}(\theta-qH)\right]\\
\theta+q\end{pmatrix}.\qed

Note that in particular this allows us to view
D_{H}\Phi
as recording the
coordinate vectors
arising from the chart
\Phi
. This is made precise in
Remark
6.31
below and depicted in
Figure
9
.

Remark 6.31

(Coordinate vectors)
.

Since
D_{H}\Phi
is the formal derivative of
\Phi
and since
D_{H}\Phi
is an invertible linear map whose image is precisely the formal tangent space
T_{H}\mathcal{B}
,
we may view
D_{H}\Phi
as recording the coordinate vectors corresponding to
\Phi
.
Indeed, for any
\mathcal{Z}=(u,\,\theta,\,q)\in T_{H}\mathcal{B}
there is a unique
(\pi,\,\mu)\in\mathbb{H}
such that

\mathcal{Z}=(D_{H}\Phi)(\pi,\,\mu)=\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\pi\\
\left(1-\frac{H}{2}\right)\partial_{3}\pi\\
-\left(1-\frac{H}{2}\right)\partial_{3}\pi\end{pmatrix}}_{=\vcentcolon e_{p}(\pi)}+\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\frac{H}{2}\mu\\
\left(1-\frac{H}{2}\right)\mu\end{pmatrix}}_{=\vcentcolon e_{M}(\mu)}

where
e_{p}
and
e_{M}
denote the coordinate “vectors” along the
p
and
M
coordinates, respectively.
Note that these are not actually vectors, but rather coordinate
operators
, namely
e_{p}:\mathring{H}^{1}\to T_{H}\mathcal{B}
and
e_{M}:L^{2}\to T_{H}\mathcal{B}

such that
T_{H}\mathcal{B}=\im e_{p}+\im e_{M}
.
This is depicted in
Figure
9
.

Figure 9
:
The “columns” of the formal derivative
D_{H}\Phi
, denoted by
e_{p}
and
e_{M}
, may be viewed as coordinate vectors on the formal tangent space.

A minor miracle now occurs: while
p
and
M
are coordinates along the balanced set
\mathcal{B}
,
their dual coordinates turn out to be, essentially,
PV
and
M
!
We see this by computing the
L^{2}_{H}
–adjoint of
D_{H}\Phi
.

Lemma 6.32
.

Note that the dual of
\mathbb{H}
is
\mathbb{H}^{*}=H^{-1}\times L^{2}
.
Equipping
T_{H}\mathcal{B}
with the inner product
L^{2}_{H}
, the adjoint
{(D_{H}\Phi)}^{*}:T_{H}\mathcal{B}\to\mathbb{H}^{*}
is given by

{(D_{H}\Phi)}^{*}(u,\,\theta,\,q)=\begin{pmatrix}-\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\cdot u_{h}-\partial_{3}\theta+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}\left[H(\theta+q)\right]\\
\left(1+\frac{H}{2}\right)(\theta+q)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-PV+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}(HM)\\
\left(1+\frac{H}{2}\right)M\end{pmatrix}.

Proof.

For any
(p,\,M)\in\mathbb{H}
and any
(u,\,\theta,\,q)\in T_{H}\mathcal{B}
we compute by integrating by parts that

\displaystyle{\left\langle D_{H}\Phi(p,\,M),\,(u,\,\theta,\,q)\right\rangle}_{L^{2}_{H}}

\displaystyle=\int u\cdot\nabla_{h}^{\perp}p+\theta\left[\partial_{3}p+\frac{H}{2}(M-\partial_{3}p)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+qH\left[M-\partial_{3}p-\frac{H}{2}(M-\partial_{3}p)\right]+(\theta+q)M

\displaystyle=\int u\cdot\nabla_{h}^{\perp}p+\theta\partial_{3}p-\frac{H}{2}(\theta+q)\partial_{3}p+\left(1+\frac{H}{2}\right)(\theta+q)M

\displaystyle=\int-(\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\cdot u_{h})p-(\partial_{3}\theta)p+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}\left[H(\theta+q)\right]+\left(1+\frac{H}{2}\right)(\theta+q)M

\displaystyle={\left\langle-\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\cdot u_{h}-\partial_{3}\theta+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}[M(\theta+q)],\,p\right\rangle}_{H^{-1}\times\mathring{H}^{1}}

\displaystyle\qquad+{\left\langle\left(1+\frac{H}{2}\right)(\theta+q),\,M\right\rangle}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle={\left\langle\begin{pmatrix}-\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\cdot u_{h}-\partial_{3}\theta+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}[H(\theta+q)]\\
\left(1+\frac{H}{2}\right)M\end{pmatrix},\,\begin{pmatrix}p\\
M\end{pmatrix}\right\rangle}_{H^{*}\times H}.\qed

Remark 6.33

(Dual coordinate vectors)
.

Just as in
Remark
6.31
we viewed
D_{H}\Phi
as recording the coordinate vectors corresponding to the chart
\Phi
,
we can view
{(D_{H}\Phi)}^{*}
as recording their dual coordinate one-forms.
Indeed, if we define, for any
(u,\,\theta,\,q)\in T_{H}\mathcal{B}
,

\displaystyle e_{p}^{\flat}(u,\,\theta,\,q)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=-PV+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}(HM)\text{ and }

\displaystyle e_{M}^{\flat}(u,\,\theta,\,q)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left(1+\frac{H}{2}\right)(\theta+q)

then we see that, for any
(u,\,\theta,\,q)\in T_{H}\mathcal{B}
and any
(\pi,\,\mu)\in\mathbb{H}
,

\displaystyle{\langle(u,\,\theta,\,q),\,e_{p}(\pi)\rangle}_{L^{2}_{H}}

\displaystyle={\langle e_{p}^{\flat}(u,\,\theta,\,q),\,p\rangle}_{\mathring{H}^{1}}\text{ and }

\displaystyle{\langle(u,\,\theta,\,q),\,e_{M}(\mu)\rangle}_{L^{2}_{H}}

\displaystyle={\langle e_{M}^{\flat}(u,\,\theta,\,q),\,M\rangle}_{L^{2}}.

This is depicted in
Figure
10
.

Figure 10
:
A depiction of the dual coordinates
e_{M}^{\flat}=\left(1+\frac{H}{2}\right)M
and

e_{p}^{\flat}=-PV+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}(HM)
on the formal tangent space
T_{H}\mathcal{B}
.

We can see that these coordinates
p
and
M
are actually orthogonal.
This is recorded below, in a result that will also come in handy when interpreting the Newton descent iteration geometrically.

Lemma 6.34
.

For any
(p,\,M)\in\mathbb{H}
,

{(D_{H}\Phi)}^{*}(D_{H}\Phi)(p,\,M)=\begin{pmatrix}-\nabla\cdot(A_{H}\nabla p)\\
\left(1+\frac{H}{2}\right)M\end{pmatrix}

for
A_{H}\vcentcolon=I-\frac{H}{2}e_{3}\otimes e_{3}
(as usual).

Remark 6.35
.

Note that
{(D_{H}\Phi)}^{*}(D_{H}\Phi)=(-\Delta)\oplus 1
when
H=0
,
which is the canonical isometry from
\mathbb{H}=\mathring{H}^{1}\times L^{2}
to its dual
H^{*}=H^{-1}\times L^{2}
.
This is because when
H=0
the dry case may be viewed as a special case of the moist Boussinesq system which corresponds to neglecting
q
(i.e. setting
q=0
).

Proof of
Lemma
6.34
.

Let
(p,\,M)\in\mathbb{H}
and let us write
(u,\,\theta,\,q)\vcentcolon=D_{H}\Phi(p,\,M)
.
Then
M(u,\,\theta,\,q)\vcentcolon=\theta+q=M
while

PV(u,\,\theta,\,q)\vcentcolon=\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\cdot u_{h}+\partial_{3}\theta=\nabla\cdot(A_{H}\nabla p)+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}(HM).

Therefore

\displaystyle{(D_{H}\Phi)}^{*}(D_{H}\Phi)(p,\,M)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-PV+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}(HM)\\
\left(1+\frac{H}{2}\right)M\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-\nabla\cdot(A_{H}\nabla p)-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}(HM)+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}(HM)\\
\left(1+\frac{H}{2}\right)M\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-\nabla\cdot(A_{H}\nabla p)\\
1+H/2\end{pmatrix}

as claimed.
∎

Remark 6.36
.

Written in block form,

{(D_{H}\Phi)}^{*}(D_{H}\Phi)=\begin{pmatrix}-\nabla\cdot(A_{H}\nabla)&0\\
0&1+H/2\end{pmatrix}

which is block-diagonal.
This means that the coordinates
p
and
M
of the Hilbert manifold
\mathcal{B}
, which arise from the global chart
\Phi
,
may formally be viewed as
L_{H}^{2}
–orthogonal (but not orthonormal).

In other words, for any
\mathcal{Z}\in T_{H}\mathcal{B}
, which may be written uniquely as

\mathcal{Z}=e_{p}(\pi)+e_{M}(\mu)

for
(\pi,\,\mu)\in\mathbb{H}
and
e_{p}
and
e_{M}
as in
Remark
6.31
, we have that

\displaystyle{\langle e_{p}(\pi),\,e_{M}(\mu)\rangle}_{L^{2}_{H}}=\int 0+\left(1-\frac{H}{2}\right)(\partial_{3}\pi)\cdot\frac{H}{2}\mu-\left(1-\frac{H}{2}\right)(\partial_{3}\pi)\cdot\left(1-\frac{H}{2}\right)\mu\cdot H+0

\displaystyle=\int\frac{H}{4}(\partial_{3}\pi)\mu-\frac{H}{4}(\partial_{3}\pi)\mu=0.

This allows us to refine our comment of
Remark
6.31
: the decomposition
T_{H}\mathcal{B}=\im e_{p}+\im e_{M}
is actually
L^{2}_{H}
–orthogonal.

Moreover, for the dual one-forms
e_{p}^{\flat}
and
e_{M}^{\flat}
as defined in
Remark
6.33
we have that

\mu=\theta+q\text{ and }\pi\text{ solves }\nabla\cdot(A_{H}\nabla\pi)=\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\cdot u_{h}+\partial_{3}\theta.

(6.26)

Indeed:

\left(1+\frac{H}{2}\right)(\theta+q)=e_{M}^{\flat}(\mathcal{Z})=e_{M}(\flat(e_{p}(\pi))+e_{M}^{\flat}(e_{M}(\mu))=0+\left(1+\frac{H}{2}\right)\mu.

Similarly: on one hand

e_{p}^{\flat}(\mathcal{Z})=-\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\cdot u_{h}-\partial_{3}\theta+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}[H(\theta+q)]=-\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\cdot u_{h}-\partial_{3}\theta+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}(HM)

(6.27)

while on the other hand

e_{p}^{\flat}(e_{p}(\pi))+e_{p}^{\flat}(e_{M}(\mu))=-\nabla\cdot(A_{H}\nabla\pi)+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}(H\mu)=-\nabla\cdot(A_{H}\nabla\pi)+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}(HM).

(6.28)

Equating (
6.27
) and (
6.28
) yields the equation
for
\pi
recorded in (
6.26
), as desired.

Remark 6.37

(Comparison of the
L^{2}_{H}
–orthogonal decomposition with well-known orthogonal decompositions)
.

In
Remark
6.33
above we have explained how any formal tangent vector
may be written in a unique way in terms of the “coordinate vectors”
e_{p}
and
e_{M}
.

More precisely, we know that for any formal tangent vector
\mathcal{Z}=(u,\,\theta,\,q)\in T_{H}\mathcal{B}

there exists a unique pair
(\pi,\,\mu)\in\mathbb{H}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}=e_{p}(\pi)+e_{M}(\mu)

\displaystyle\text{ where }\left\{\begin{aligned} &e_{M}^{\flat}(e_{M}(\mu))=e_{M}^{\flat}(\mathcal{Z})\text{ and }\\
&e_{p}^{\flat}(e_{p}(\pi))=e_{p}^{\flat}(\mathcal{Z})\end{aligned}\right.

(6.29)

\displaystyle\iff\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mu=\theta+q\text{ and }\\
&\pi\text{ solves }\nabla\cdot(A_{H}\nabla\pi)=PV.\end{aligned}\right.

We will now help the reader make better sense of this by showing how their favourite orthogonal decomposition,
namely that of Fourier series, may be written in this way.

Fourier series tell us that every
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
has uniquely determined coordinates
{(a_{k})}_{k}\in l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{3})
such that

f(x)=\sum_{k}a_{k}e^{2\pi ik\cdot x}.

(6.30)

Given a function
f
, the process producing its Fourier series can be split into two steps.

1.

We compute the amplitudes
a_{k}
.

2.

We reconstruct
f
from its amplitudes.

Let us rewrite each of these two steps in a manner which will make it easier to directly compare with the
L^{2}_{H}
–orthogonal decomposition discussed above.

•

Step 1: The computation of each amplitude corresponds to a map
a_{k}:L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})\to\mathbb{C}
defined by

a_{k}(f)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}f(x)e^{-2\pi ik\cdot x}dx.

•

Step 2: We write the reconstruction step in an admittedly unusual manner.
This will make it easier to compare Fourier series to the
L^{2}_{H}
–orthogonal decomposition of our formal tangent vectors.

If we view each
a_{k}
as corresponding to a coordinate direction in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
we may then encode this coordinate direction as a map

e_{k}:\mathbb{C}\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
given by

[e_{k}(a)](x)=ae^{2\pi ik\cdot k}.

Combining these formulations of the two steps used to produce Fourier series we may write (
6.30
) in the form

f=\sum_{k}e_{k}(a_{k})\text{ where }a_{k}={\langle f,\,e^{2\pi ik\cdot x}\rangle}_{L^{2}}.

(6.31)

In particular, in the language we used to describe the formal tangent space
T_{H}\mathcal{B}
,
each “coordinate vector”
e_{k}
has a “dual coordinate vector”
e_{k}^{\flat}
, which is really just its adjoint and is a map

e_{k}^{\flat}:L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})\to\mathbb{C}
.
Using the
L^{2}
inner product on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
and the standard complex inner product on
\mathbb{C}
defined by
{\langle x,\,y\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}}=x\bar{y}

for every
x,\,y\in\mathbb{C}
we may compute these dual coordinates vectors:
for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
, any
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
, and any
a\in\mathbb{C}
,

\displaystyle{\langle e_{k}^{\flat}(f),\,a\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}}={\langle f,\,e_{k}(a)\rangle}_{L^{2}}=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}f(x)\overline{ae^{2\pi ik\cdot x}}dx=\bar{a}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}f(x)e^{-2\pi ik\cdot x}dx=\bar{a}\,a_{k}(f)={\langle a_{k}(f),\,a\rangle}_{\mathbb{C}}.

In other words:
e_{k}^{\flat}=a_{k}
.

We may thus write (
6.31
), already an alternate formulation of (
6.30
),
in a manner even more reminiscent of our treatment of coordinate vectors and dual coordinate vectors in
T_{H}\mathcal{B}
, namely

f=\sum_{k}e_{k}(a_{k})\text{ where }a_{k}=e_{k}^{\flat}(f).

(6.32)

Finally, to turn this expression into an exact parallel of our treatment of
T_{H}\mathcal{B}

we must use the orthonormality of the Fourier basis. That is, we use the fact that

e_{k}^{\flat}(e_{k}(a))=a_{k}(e_{k}(a))=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}ae^{2\pi ik\cdot x}e^{-2\pi ik\cdot x}dx=a.

We may therefore rewrite (
6.32
) in its final form as

f=\sum_{k}e_{k}(a_{k})\text{ where }e_{k}^{\flat}(e_{k}(a_{k}))=e_{k}^{\flat}(f).

(6.33)

This is exactly the same form as (
6.29
)!

This section is concerned with the geometry of the balanced set
\mathcal{B}
on its own,
and not with how it is embedded in the broader state space
\mathbb{L}^{2}_{\sigma}
.
Nonetheless, at this stage it is worth commenting on how the
L^{2}_{H}
inner product endowed on the formal tangent space
T_{H}\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{B}

interacts with the wave set.
The result below is depicted in
Figure
11
.

Lemma 6.38
.

The wave set
\mathcal{W}
is
L^{2}_{H}
–orthogonal to the formal tangent space
T_{H}\mathcal{B}
in the sense that,
for any
\mathcal{Z}\in T_{H}\mathcal{B}
and any
\mathcal{Y}\in\mathcal{W}
,
{\langle\mathcal{Z},\,\mathcal{Y}\rangle}_{L^{2}_{H}}=0
.

Proof.

Lemma
6.28
tells us that
\mathcal{Z}=D_{H}\Phi(\pi,\,\mu)

for some
(\pi,\,mu)\in\mathbb{H}

while
Proposition
3.3
tells us that
\mathcal{Y}=\Psi(\sigma,\,w)
for some
\sigma\in\mathring{H}^{1}_{\sigma}
and
w\in L^{2}
.
Then

\displaystyle{\langle\mathcal{Z},\,\mathcal{Y}\rangle}_{L^{2}_{H}}

\displaystyle={\langle D_{H}\Phi(\pi,\,M),\,\Psi(\sigma,\,w)\rangle}_{L^{2}_{H}}

\displaystyle=\int\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\pi\cdot\sigma_{h}^{\perp}+\left(1-\frac{H}{2}\right)(\partial_{3}\pi)\cdot\sigma_{3}+\left(1-\frac{H}{2}\right)(\partial_{3}\pi)\cdot\sigma_{3}\cdot H+0

\displaystyle=\int\nabla_{h}\pi\cdot\sigma_{h}+(\partial_{3}\pi)\sigma_{3}=\int\nabla\pi\cdot\sigma=0

since
\sigma
is divergence-free.
∎

\mathcal{B}
p
M
T_{H}\mathcal{B}
\mathcal{Z}=D_{H}\Phi(\pi,\,\mu)
\mathcal{W}
\mathcal{Y}=\Psi(\xi,\,w)
\mathcal{X}

Figure 11
:
Depiction of
Lemma
6.38
: the wave set is
L^{2}_{H}
–orthogonal
to the formal tangent space to the balanced set.

We are now ready to use the geometric language introduced in this section in order
to provide a geometric interpretation of the Newton descent iteration presented in
Section
6.1
.

First, we note that we can rewrite the defining equation of the Newton descent search direction, namely (
6.8
), as follows.

Remark 6.39
.

The Newton descent direction
\pi
solves, for
H\vcentcolon=\mathds{1}(M<\partial_{3}p)
,

-\nabla\cdot(A_{H}\nabla\pi)=PV(\Phi(p,\,M))-PV.

This follows from (
6.8
) upon observing that
\min_{0}(M-\partial_{3}p)=H(M-\partial_{3}p)
.

In other words, and this will play a key role in the geometric interpretation of Newton descent:

\pi
is determined by the potential vorticity discrepancy, i.e. by how far the potential vorticity at the current guess is from the true potential vorticity.

We are now ready to describe the geometric interpretation of Newton descent.

Remark 6.40

(Geometric interpretation of Newton descent)
.

Consider the following formal flow on the balanced set
\mathcal{B}
and its formal tangent space
T_{H}\mathcal{B}
.

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\dot{\mathcal{X}}(s)={(D_{H}\Phi)}^{-*}\left(PV-PV(\mathcal{X}),\,0\right)\text{ and }\\
&\mathcal{X}(0)=\Phi(p_{0},\,M)\end{aligned}\right.

(6.34)

where
PV
and
M
are given and where the starting point
p_{0}\in\mathring{H}^{1}
may be freely chosen.

We claim that, formally, the Newton descent iteration may be viewed as a discretisation of this flow.
First: what does this flow even do?

Well, since

e_{p}^{\flat}=-PV+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}(HM)\text{ and }e_{M}^{\flat}=\left(1+\frac{H}{2}\right)M

we may view
PV
and
M
as dual coordinates along the balanced set
\mathcal{B}
.
The map
{(D_{H}\Phi)}^{-*}:\mathbb{H}^{*}\to T_{H}\mathcal{B}
thus associates to
(PV,\,M)\in\mathbb{H}^{*}

the corresponding tangent vector in
T_{H}\mathcal{B}
.
This means that the flow follows the
PV
–direction, keeping the value of
M
fixed, so as to approach the given target value of
PV
.
This is depicted in
Figure
12
.
(An analog of this flow is presented in
Remark
6.41
below in a much simpler setting in order to make the intuition easier to follow.)

To see that the Newton iteration is a discretisation of this flow we rewrite the Newton iteration in a different way.

The Newton iteration proceed as follows.

•

PV
and
M
are given.

•

Initialize the iteration by choosing an arbitrary
p_{0}\in\mathring{H}^{1}
.

•

Iterate, for
k\geqslant 0
,

1.

Let
H_{k}\vcentcolon=\mathds{1}(M<\partial_{3}p_{k})

and
{PV}_{k}\vcentcolon=\Delta p_{k}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}{\min}_{0}\,(M-\partial_{3}p_{k})
.

2.

\pi
solves
\nabla\cdot(A_{H_{k}}\nabla\pi)=PV-{PV}_{k}
.

3.

Let
p_{k+1}\vcentcolon=p_{k}+\pi
.

Note that step 3 is written this way because, once the Newton iteration reaches the so-called “quadratic regime”
(which is guaranteed to occur once iterates are sufficiently close to the true solution),
then exact line search always produces a unit step size. This is why we can update
p_{k+1}=p_{k}+t\pi
with
t=1
.

We are now ready to relate the Newton iteration to the flow above.
We may interpret (really, rewrite) the Newton iteration geometrically as follows.

•

PV
and
M
are given.

•

Initialize the iteration by choosing an arbitrary
p_{0}\in\mathring{H}^{1}
,
i.e. choosing an arbitrary
\mathcal{X}_{0}=\Phi(p_{0},\,M)\in\mathcal{B}

with the correct
M
–coordinate.

•

Iterate, for
k\geqslant 0
.

1.

Let
(p_{k},\,M)\vcentcolon=\Phi^{-1}(\mathcal{X}_{k})

and
{PV}_{k}\vcentcolon=PV(\mathcal{X}_{k})
.

2.

\pi
solves
{(D_{H}\Phi)}^{*}(D_{H}\Phi)(\pi,\,0)=\left(PV-{PV}_{k},\,0\right)
.

3.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{k+1}\vcentcolon=\Phi\left((p_{k},\,M)+(\pi,\,0)\right)
.

Note that step 2 is the same as above, merely written differently (actually: every step coincides!)
Indeed
Lemma
6.34
tells us that

{(D_{H}\Phi)}^{*}(D_{H}\Phi)(\pi,\,0)=\left(-\nabla\cdot(A_{H}\nabla\pi),\,0\right).

In other words the Newton iteration may be written in one line as

\mathcal{X}_{k+1}=\Phi\left(\Phi^{-1}(\mathcal{X}_{k})+(\pi,\,0)\right)\text{ where $\pi$ solves }{(D_{H}\Phi)}^{*}(D_{H}\Phi)(\pi,\,0)=\left(PV-{PV}_{k},\,0\right).

When
\mathcal{X}_{k+1}
is close to
\mathcal{X}_{k}
, which equivalently means that
\pi
is small, we may thus formally approximate

\mathcal{X}_{k+1}\sim\mathcal{X}_{k}+(D_{H}\Phi)(\pi,\,0).

We compute that

\displaystyle(D_{H}\Phi)(\pi,\,0)=(D_{H}\Phi){\left[{(D_{H}\Phi)}^{*}(D_{H}\Phi)\right]}^{-1}\left(PV-{PV}_{k},\,0\right)

\displaystyle={(D_{H}\Phi)}^{-*}\left(PV-PV(\mathcal{X}_{k}),\,0\right)

In other words:

\mathcal{X}_{k+1}\sim\mathcal{X}_{k}+{(D_{H}\Phi)}^{-*}\left(PV-PV(\mathcal{X}_{k}),\,0\right)

which verifies that indeed the Newton iteration is a formal discretisation of the flow in (
6.34
) above.

Figure 12
:
The Newton descent method follows curves of constant
M
until the appropriate value of
PV
is reached: at a point
\mathcal{X}
on the balanced set, the flow corresponding to Newton descent follows the direction of the formal vector
\mathcal{Z}
whose
p
–coordinate is given by the Newton descent search direction
\pi
.

Remark 6.41

(A simple flow)
.

We discuss here a very simple analog of the flow presented in
Remark
6.40
.
Consider the following flow on the two-dimensional plane:

\displaystyle\dot{x}(s)=x_{*}-x(s)

\displaystyle\dot{y}(s)=0

with initial conditions

\displaystyle x(0)=x_{0}

\displaystyle y(0)=y_{*}

where
x_{*}
and
y_{*}
are given (they play the role of the given target values of
PV
and
M
) and where
x_{0}
(just like
p_{0}
) may be freely chosen.
This means that the
x
–coordinate plays the role of the
PV
–coordinate whereas the
y
–coordinate plays the role of the
M
–coordinate.

Then the new unknown

\tilde{x}(s)\vcentcolon=x_{*}-x(s)

satisfies

\dot{\tilde{x}}(s)=-\dot{x}(s)=-\dot{\tilde{x}}(s)

and so we deduce that the solution is given by

\displaystyle\tilde{x}(s)=\tilde{x}(0)e^{-s}=(x_{*}-x_{0})e^{-s}

\displaystyle y(s)=y_{*}.

In terms of the original unknowns
x
and
y
this yields

\displaystyle x(s)=x_{*}-\tilde{x}(s)=(1-e^{-s})x_{*}+e^{-s}x_{0}

\displaystyle y(s)=y_{*}.

Therefore the solution
(x,\,y)(s)
approaches the point with coordinates
(x_{*},\,y_{*})
exponentially fast,
along a trajectory of constant
y
.

7
Summary and comparison with Helmholtz and the dry case

We are approaching the conclusion of this paper.
Before we conclude, we recall in this section that the decomposition we introduced for the moist Boussinesq system under consideration
has many different facets.
We summarize these many facets here.
In order to make it as easy as possible for the reader to follow this discussion, we first recall the many facets of the Helmholtz decomposition
and of the vortical-wave decomposition of the dry Boussinesq system.

This is not merely a recapitulation of the discussion of these two classical systems done in the introduction.
Throughout the paper we have introduced a variety of concepts and terminology for the sake of the moist decomposition,
but we see that many of these have counterparts in these classical systems.
It is these counterparts that we summarize first, in
Sections
7.1
and
7.2
below,
before summarizing the various facets of the moist decomposition in
Section
7.3
.

We could more prosaically call this section: “the twelve aspects of a decomposition found in both Helmholtz, dry, and moist Boussinesq”.

7.1
Helmholtz

We first summarize the various facets of the Helmholtz decomposition.

1.

For any three-dimensional vector field
u
the Helmholtz decomposition is

u=\nabla p+\sigma

where
\sigma
is a divergence-free vector field.

2.

In the context of the Helmholtz decomposition, if we wish to use terminology from atmospheric science and talk about
balanced states
,
a state
u
will be balanced precisely when it is a solenoidal vector field.
In other words the balance in question is

u_{B}=\sigma,

where the subscript
B
denotes the balanced component of
u
.

While introducing this terminology in the context of the Helmholtz decomposition may appear overzealous,
it does serve to make the parallels with the dry Boussinesq system, and ultimately with the
moist
Boussinesq system, more evident.

3.

Since the second component of the Helmholtz decomposition is the balanced component,
by analogy with the terminology
of this paper we refer to the first component as the
wave component
2
2

2

This terminology may also be justified more directly as follows, without appealing to how the Helmholtz decomposition is similar to the dry and moist Boussinesq decompositions.
Indeed, the Helmholtz decomposition may be interpreted as a slow-fast decomposition for the low Mach limit of the compressible Euler equations (see
Remark
A.3
) and in this context the first component of the decomposition will precisely obey a wave equation.

.
By analogy with
Proposition
3.3
we may identify the
slow measurement
in this setting as the measurement which annihilates the wave set.
Since the wave set is the set of potential vector fields this means that the slow measurements are precisely the
curl
and the
spatial average
:

\\
nablap
is the wave component of
u
and

\nabla\times\nabla p=0\text{ and }\fint\nabla p=0.

4.

In this case there is no subtlety to identifying the “good unknown” since it is the vector field
u
itself. (See
Remark
3.2
for a discussion of what is meant by the term “good unknown” and what role they play.)

5.

The elliptic PDE used to reconstruct the balanced component can be viewed as a div-curl system.
Indeed
\sigma
solves

\nabla\times\sigma=\nabla\times u\text{ subject to }\nabla\cdot\sigma=0\text{ and }\fint\sigma=\fint u.

Note that the forcing is precisely the slow measurements: here the curl and the spatial average are the analogs of the potential vorticity in the dry case
since they are slow measurements which characterise the balanced component.

Alternatively we can view this div-curl system as a curl-squared system for the vector potential of
\sigma
.
Writing
\sigma=c+\nabla\times\psi
we see that
c=\fint u
while
\psi
solves
\nabla\times(\nabla\times\psi)=\nabla\times u
.
(This curl-squared system may be written as a Laplacian equation since we may without loss of generality impose that the vector potential
\psi
be divergence-free,
and in that case the curl-squared operator acting on
\psi
reduces to a Laplacian.)

6.

The elliptic PDE used to reconstruct the wave component is Laplace’s equation:

\Delta p=\nabla\cdot u.

Note that it is not the fast measurement, the divergence, which plays the role of a forcing term for the wave component.

7.

Using the decomposition we can obtain a couple of global changes of coordinates.
We can fully describe the vector field
u
in three ways.

(a)

Describe it using
u
itself.

(b)

Describe it using the measurements
\nabla\cdot u
,
\nabla\times u
, and
\fint u
.

(c)

Describe it using the coordinates
p
and
\sigma
.

8.

Recall that the Helmholtz decomposition may be viewed as a slow-fast decomposition for the low Mach number limit of the compressible Euler equations.
In that case the leading-order operator of the dynamics is linear, and it is recorded in
Remark
A.3
.
We bring this up now in order to later compare the leading order operators in the case of dry and moist Boussinesq.
For now it suffices to note that, as is discussed in more detail in
Remark
A.3
,
the Helmholtz decomposition is a decomposition into the kernel and the image for that operator: the balanced set is its kernel and the wave set is its image.

9.

As discussed in
Remark
A.3
, the wave component of the Helmholtz decomposition
can be seen to obey a wave equation.
Once again, this is not necessarily worthy of note by itself, but we record it here to compare it with the dry and moist Boussinesq decompositions
in
Sections
7.2
and
7.3
below.

10.

When viewed as a slow-fast decomposition, the Helmholtz decomposition inherits the additional feature of being orthogonal with respect to the conserved energy,
which happens to simply be the
L^{2}
norm.

11.

We note that the Helmholtz decomposition and its induced changes of coordinates discussed above yield Parseval–type identities which allow us to rewrite
the energy in three different ways: in terms of the state
u
, in terms of the coordinates, and in terms of the measurements. We obtain that

\displaystyle\int{\lvert u\rvert}^{2}=\int{\lvert\nabla p\rvert}^{2}+{\lvert\sigma\rvert}^{2}=\int{\lvert\nabla\Delta^{-1}(\nabla\cdot u)\rvert}^{2}+{\left\lvert{\nabla\times}^{-1}\left(\nabla\times u,\,\fint u\right)\right\rvert}^{2}.

Here we write
\sigma={(\nabla\times)}^{-1}(\omega,\,a)
to mean that
\sigma
solves

\nabla\times\sigma=\omega\text{ subject to }\nabla\cdot\sigma=0\text{ and }\fint\sigma=a.

12.

Finally we observe that the extraction of the balanced component of a vector field
u
may be viewed as a projection. Indeed, if we define the balanced set, in the context of the Helmholtz decomposition, to be
\mathcal{B}_{Hel.}\vcentcolon={(L^{2})}^{3}_{\sigma}
, the space of divergence-free three-dimensional vector fields,
then we see that

u_{B}=\argmin_{v\in\mathcal{B}_{Hel.}}{\left\lvert\left\lvert u-v\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{L^{2}}.

7.2
Dry Boussinesq

We now turn our attention to the various facets of the vortical-wave decomposition used for the dry Boussinesq system.
Note that the numbering below is the same as the numbering in the discussion of the Helmholtz decomposition in
Section
7.1
above.
That is not an accident: it serves to highlight the parallels between the two decompositions.
Then again we caution the reader that the roles played by the potential and solenoidal pieces are now flipped:
\nabla p
appeared in the fast waves in
Section
7.1
, it now appears in the slow balanced components;
\sigma
appeared in the slow balanced components in
Section
7.1
, it now appears in the fast waves.

1.

For the dry Boussinesq system discussed in this paper, the decomposition of a state consisting of a vector velocity field
u
and a scalar potential temperature field
\theta

takes the form

\begin{pmatrix}u\\
\theta\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{h}^{\perp}p\\
\partial_{3}p\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{h}^{\perp}+we_{3}\\
\sigma_{3}\end{pmatrix},

where
\sigma
is divergence-free.

2.

In the dry case, the balanced component satisfies the geostrophic and hydrostatic balances:

\displaystyle u_{B}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{h}^{\perp}p\text{ and }

\displaystyle\theta_{B}

\displaystyle=\partial_{3}p.

3.

The measurement characterising the wave set is, in the dry case, the potential vorticity.
Indeed in this case the wave set is precisely the set of states whose potential vorticity vanishes:

\displaystyle PV(\sigma_{h}^{\perp}+we_{3},\,\sigma_{3})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\cdot{(\sigma_{h}^{\perp}+we_{3})}_{h}+\partial_{3}\sigma_{3}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{h}\cdot\sigma_{h}+\partial_{3}\sigma_{3}=0

since
\sigma
is divergence-free.

4.

In the dry case the good unknown is the following three-dimensional vector field combining both the horizontal velocity and the potential temperature:

-u_{h}^{\perp}+\theta e_{3}.

5.

The elliptic equation used to recover the pressure
p
, and hence the balanced component, is the Laplace/Poisson equation

\Delta p=PV.

6.

The wave component is recovered by setting
w=u_{3}
and finding
\sigma
to be the solution of the following div-curl system:

\nabla\times\sigma=\partial_{3}u_{h}-\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\theta+(\partial_{3}w)e_{3}=j+(\partial_{3}w)e_{3}\text{ subject to }\nabla\cdot\sigma=0\text{ and }\fint\sigma=-\fint u_{h}^{\perp}+\fint\theta e_{3}=a.

7.

We may describe states of the dry Boussinesq system in three equivalent ways.

(a)

Describe them using
u
and
\theta
.

(b)

Describe them using the measurements
PV
,
j
,
w
, and
a
.

(c)

Describe them using the coordinates
p
,
\sigma
, and
w
.

8.

The leading-order operator of the dynamics of the dry Boussinesq system considered in this paper is given by

\mathcal{L}\begin{pmatrix}u\\
\theta\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{P}_{L}\left(u_{h}^{\perp}-\theta e_{3}\right)\\
u_{3}\end{pmatrix}.

The decomposition is adapted to this operator since the balanced set is its kernel and the wave set is its image.

9.

We may compute immediately that the wave component of a state undergoes linear oscillations under the leading-order operator since, when restricted to the wave set, the square of the operator behaves like multiplication by
-1
:

\mathcal{L}^{2}\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{h}^{\perp}+we_{3}\\
\sigma_{3}\end{pmatrix}=-\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{h}^{\perp}+we_{3}\\
\sigma_{3}\end{pmatrix}.

10.

The conserved energy in the dry case is simply the
L^{2}
norm given by

E=\int{\lvert u\rvert}^{2}+\theta^{2}.

11.

The Parseval-type identity in the dry case lets us rewrite the energy in terms of the coordinates or the measurements as follows:

\int{\lvert u\rvert}^{2}+\theta^{2}=\int{\lvert\nabla p\rvert}^{2}+{\lvert\sigma\rvert}^{2}+w^{2}=\int{\lvert\nabla\Delta^{-1}PV\rvert}^{2}+{\left\lvert{\nabla\times}^{-1}\left(j+(\partial_{3}w)e_{3},\,a\right)\right\rvert}^{2}.

12.

For the dry Boussinesq case the balanced set takes the form

\mathcal{B}_{dry}\vcentcolon=\left\{(u,\,\theta)\in{(L^{2})}^{3}_{\sigma}\times L^{2}:u=\nabla_{h}^{\perp}p,\,u_{3}=0,\text{ and }\theta=\partial_{3}p\text{ for some }p\in\mathring{H}^{1}\right\}.

Therefore the extraction of a balanced component once again has a projection perspective:

(u_{B},\,\theta_{B})=\argmin_{\mathcal{Y}\in\mathcal{B}_{dry}}{\left\lvert\left\lvert(u,\,\theta)-\mathcal{Y}\right\rvert\right\rvert}_{L^{2}}.

7.3
Moist Boussinesq

Finally we perform the same exercise as in
Sections
7.1
and
7.2
:
we list the twelve aspects of the moist decomposition presented in this paper, in the same order as the above sections to make the comparison easy to follow.

Recall that here, in a manner consistent with the rest of this paper (barring the introduction) we write
\theta
to denote the
equivalent
potential temperature
(denoted
\theta_{e}
in the introduction).

1.

The decomposition of a state
(u,\,\theta,q)
is given by

\begin{pmatrix}u\\
\theta\\
q\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\\
\partial_{3}p+\frac{1}{2}{\min}_{0}\,(M-\partial_{3}p)\\
M-\partial_{3}p-\frac{1}{2}{\min}_{0}\,(M-\partial_{3}p)\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{h}^{\perp}+we_{3}\\
\sigma_{3}\\
-\sigma_{3}\end{pmatrix},

where
p
has vanishing average and
\sigma
is divergence-free.

2.

In the moist case the balanced component satisfies the geostrophic and hydrostatic balances, meaning that

\displaystyle u_{B}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{h}^{\perp}p\text{ and }

\displaystyle\theta_{B}-{\min}_{0}\,q_{B}

\displaystyle=\partial_{3}p.

To fully characterise the balanced component we must also include the moist variable
M
and the following “auxiliary” balance:

\theta_{B}+q_{B}=M.

3.

The slow measurements characterising the wave set are now
PV
and
M
, and indeed

PV(\sigma_{h}^{\perp}+we_{3},\,\sigma_{3},\,-\sigma_{3})=\nabla\cdot\sigma=0\text{ and }M(\sigma_{h}^{\perp}+we_{3},\,\sigma_{3},\,-\sigma_{3})=\sigma_{3}-\sigma_{3}=0.

4.

There are
two
good unknowns in the moist case, owing to the fact that the leading-order operator is
not
skew-symmetric (this is discussed in more detail in
Remark
3.8
).
One good unknown is

-u_{h}^{\perp}+(\theta-{\min}_{0}\,q)e_{3},

which is related to
j
and the null set of the leading-order operator,
while its “adjoint” good unknown is

-u_{h}^{\perp}+\theta e_{3},

which is related to
PV
and the image of the leading-order operator.

5.

In order to reconstruct the balanced component we set
M=\theta+q
and then let
p
be the solution of nonlinear
PV
-and-
M
inversion

\Delta p+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}{\min}_{0}\,(M-\partial_{3}p)=PV.

6.

To reconstruct the wave component we let
w=u_{3}
and let
\sigma
solve

\displaystyle\nabla\times\left(A_{B}^{-1}\sigma\right)=\partial_{3}u_{h}-\nabla_{h}^{\perp}(\theta-\theta H_{B})+(\partial_{3}w)e_{3}=\vcentcolon j_{B}+(\partial_{3}w)e_{3}

\displaystyle\text{ subject to }\nabla\cdot\sigma=0\text{ and }\fint A_{B}^{-1}\sigma=-\fint u_{h}^{\perp}+\fint(\theta-qH_{B})e_{3},

where
A_{B}^{-1}=I+H_{B}e_{3}\otimes e_{3}
for
H_{B}\vcentcolon=\mathds{1}(M<\partial_{3}p)
.

7.

We can describe moist Boussinesq states in three ways.

(a)

Describe them using
u
,
\theta
, and
q
.

(b)

Describe them using the measurements
PV
,
M
,
j
,
w
, and
a
.

(c)

Describe them using the coordinates
p
,
M
,
\sigma
, and
w
.

8.

The leading-order operator of the dynamics in the moist case is

\mathcal{N}\begin{pmatrix}u\\
\theta\\
